Two consequences. **Approvals are now measured**: saying yes to a move sends Jefe off to change the real store, and that wait — yes → outcome on screen — was untimed and is now reported separately from chat replies, because one p50 across both would describe neither. **And the LLM ledger stops under-reporting its worst cases**: it timed each call from whenever that provider's attempt began, so a failover that burned eight seconds on Groq and two on Gemini recorded two, while the eight sat in an error row with no duration at all — the slowest turns, the ones a merchant actually noticed, were the ones we couldn't see. Calls are now timed from the ask: the answering row carries the whole wait, the failed attempt carries what it cost, and a call that fails outright is recorded instead of vanishing. Rows written before today keep their nulls, so older percentiles are a floor rather than a number. `app/lib/llm/provider.server.js`, `app/components/chat-turn-reporter.tsx`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/routes/api.chat-turn.tsx`, `apps/ops/server.mjs`, `tests/llm-provider-fallback.test.mjs`, `tests/chat-turn-latency.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe now clears dead listings off your storefront for you — Tidy-ups is live.** Products still listed with no stock left anywhere that haven't sold in six months: Jefe finds them, shows you the list, and archives them once you approve. Nothing archives on its own — the dial starts on Approve, and Autonomous is yours to choose. Every archive is reversible, capped at 50 products a run, and re-checked against the live store right before it writes, so a product you've restocked in the meantime is left alone. Founder flipped `PRODUCT_STATUS_EXECUTE_ENABLED` on 2026-08-13; verified that flag reaches only this action, because it is shared with the product-status adapter and `wireTidyUpExecution` is currently its only caller. `apps/shopify` Railway env, `app/lib/actions/action-intent.server.js`, `app/lib/actions/product-status-adapter.server.js`.

- **`/health` now says which actions can actually write to a store.** Every go-live so far was an env-var flip nobody could confirm from outside — the running process reads its own env and nothing surfaced the answer, so "it's live" meant "we deployed and hoped". `checks.actions` reports one boolean per registered action type, built from the engine's own `listActionTypes` rather than the raw environment, so no secret is exposed and a changelog entry claiming "live" can be checked in one request. `app/routes/health.tsx`, `tests/tidy-up-wiring.test.mjs`.

- **We can now say how long Jefe takes to answer — including the part the merchant actually feels.** Asked "how long did that reply take", the honest answer was that nobody knew: we recorded latency per model call, but a single "hey Jefe" is several calls plus context retrieval plus two writes, so the calls we timed both understated the wait and couldn't explain it — and nothing surfaced any of it anywhere a human looks. A turn is now timed end to end from two vantage points: Jefe's own share, split into deciding / retrieving / generating / persisting, and Send → reply on screen as measured in the browser, which is the only number a merchant experiences and the one that moves when the cost is in the round trip rather than in Jefe thinking. Both land as PII-free `chat_turn` events (durations only, never message text) and read back in two places: `/health → chatTurns` for "is it slow right now" on this instance, and an ops-panel "Chat reply latency · 7d" panel for the trend plus where the time goes. Every way a merchant starts a turn is wired — composer, suggestion chips, retry, and the per-move action chat — because a composer that clears without starting the clock would quietly drop its turns out of the numbers. Also documents a hole this exposed in the older ledger: on a provider fallback, `llm_usage_event.latency_ms` records only the fallback call, so the slowest turns under-report there. `app/lib/observability/chat-turn-latency.server.js`, `app/components/chat-turn-reporter.tsx`, `app/routes/api.chat-turn.tsx`, `app/lib/merchant-memory/general-chat.server.js`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/routes/health.tsx`, `apps/ops/server.mjs`, `docs/observability.md`, `tests/chat-turn-latency.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe can now tidy dead listings off your storefront — built, tested and deliberately still dark.** The third Settings → Autonomy row, "Tidy-ups", has shown SOON since the roster landed and had nothing behind it. It now has a full path: Jefe finds live products with no stock left anywhere that have sold nothing for six months, proposes archiving them, and — when the founder flips `PRODUCT_STATUS_EXECUTE_ENABLED` — executes through the existing typed product-status adapter, reversible to ACTIVE. **The Settings dial stays on SOON and nothing writes to a store until that flag is on — but Jefe can now propose a tidy-up from today**, as advice with no Approve button, alongside how to do it by hand. Three refusals carry the safety, each tested: a store whose own order history is shorter than the window proposes **nothing** (otherwise day one of an install proposes archiving the entire catalogue, since every product has "no sales in 180 days" when Jefe has only watched for three weeks); a variant with no ingested inventory level is **unknown, not zero**; and any positive stock anywhere disqualifies the product outright — a slow seller with stock is dead stock, which is clearance's job, not this one. Registered as unsuited to made-to-order businesses, whose whole catalogue would otherwise look stale to a stock-based test. Uses `write_products`, already granted and already exercised — no new consent. `app/lib/actions/stale-listing-tidy-up.server.js`, `app/lib/actions/wire-tidy-up-execution.server.js`, `app/lib/actions/action-intent.server.js`, `app/lib/actions/action-resolution.server.js`, `app/lib/actions/execute-approved-action.server.js`, `tests/stale-listing-tidy-up.test.mjs`, `tests/tidy-up-wiring.test.mjs`.

- **You can now name your chats.** A chat took its name from whatever you happened to type first, so a thread about Christmas stock was called "hey jefe how're you" forever, and the Chats list was a row of openers rather than a list of subjects. The chat title is now a permanent inline editor with a stable bordered input; leaving the title saves it, Enter commits it, and Escape reverts it. Clearing the box hands it back to the automatic name. Renaming doesn't reload the thread, so you don't lose your place, and Jefe never overwrites a name you chose. `app/lib/merchant-memory/general-chat.server.js`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `tests/chat-rename.test.mjs`.

### Changed

- **What Jefe knows is now a searchable settings screen instead of a short memory list.** Merchants reach it from Settings as before, but it now matches the Settings layout, uses the same bordered content panel and wider settings row as Autonomy, removes the old Memory-only global wrapper, groups every active merchant-visible belief by category, and keeps the screen to search plus the belief list. Long sentence-style beliefs stack under the source line instead of squeezing into the right-value column. Internal diagnostics and Jefe-only telemetry stay hidden. `app/components/merchant-memory-view.tsx`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/routes/app.settings.tsx`, `app/styles/jefe.css`, `tests/merchant-memory-view-composer.test.mjs`, `tests/surface-reachability.test.mjs`.

- **The glossary now shows how Jefe's pieces fit together.** Added a relationship map to `apps/shopify/GLOSSARY.md` so operators can see the spine from Shopify data and merchant input through Merchant Memory, recommendations, safe execution, the ledger and outcome learning before reading the individual definitions.

- **Store understanding now uses the memory model input budget instead of the old small prompt cap.** The store-understanding pass was still capped at 6k input tokens even after memory-grade work moved to Gemini with an 18k local budget, so a normal post-reset store read could fail before the model was called. It now reads the same `store_understanding` LLM config as the provider route, keeping the app-side guard and the selected model in agreement. `app/lib/merchant-memory/store-understanding.server.js`, `tests/store-understanding.test.mjs`.

- **New env vars now have a written release rule.** Agent instructions now require every new env var to be added to the relevant `.env.example`, and any production-required variable introduced on `main` must be synced to Railway production during release or explicitly marked as not needed there. `AGENTS.md`.

- **When merchants ask which products drove revenue, Jefe now forces a product-level read instead of accepting a total-only answer.** The coverage foundation proved Jefe had the evidence, but the planner could still choose only a revenue total and then say product-level revenue was unavailable. Product-driver questions now always carry a product ranking by line revenue, and a reply that denies product-level data is rejected when those rows exist, so the manual checkout question "Which products drove revenue last month?" returns products rather than a false limitation. `app/lib/merchant-memory/commerce-analyst.server.js`, `tests/commerce-analyst.test.mjs`.

- **The Tidy-ups setting now says what it actually does.** It read "Missing types, broken links, unclaimed refunds" — three things Jefe does not do there. Missing types is the Listing copy row below it; broken links needs redirect data Jefe doesn't ingest and a permission the app was never granted; and refund writes can't be undone, which puts them in a different class altogether. It now reads "Hides live products with nothing left to sell", which is the thing behind the dial. `app/components/settings/AutonomyPanel.tsx`.

- **The "See everything Jefe knows →" link is off the home for good.** Matt asked for it gone twice: the quiet chat-log home shouldn't carry a footer link, and Merchant Memory is reached from the shell gear → Settings → "Everything Jefe knows". It *was* removed (`63d0337`) and then silently came back with the holistic-memory rebase in #81, which is why a guard now fails the build if it reappears — the reachability tests that already prove there's a way *in* to Memory make pinning its absence on the home safe. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `tests/surface-reachability.test.mjs`.

### Fixed

- **Voice transcription model tests no longer inherit a copied local `.env` fallback model.** The voice transcription helper accepts an env override but still read the process-wide LLM config for its default fallback, so local workspaces with `LLM_FALLBACK_MODEL` set could fail an otherwise isolated test. It now threads the supplied env through the config read. `app/lib/llm/transcribe-voice.server.js`, `tests/voice-feedback.test.mjs`.

- **Chat replies no longer throw the merchant back to the top of the conversation.** Sending or retrying a store chat, and asking a follow-up inside an action chat, now preserves the reader's scroll position across the route refresh. If they were already at the bottom, Jefe keeps them pinned to the new reply; if they were reading higher up the thread, Jefe leaves them there instead of snapping to the top. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `tests/home-nav-guard.test.mjs`.

## 2026-08-12

### Fixed

- **Merchant Memory stops listing Jefe's own notes about itself as things you told it.** How often you act on Jefe's recommendations, how its clearances performed, and why you turned proposals down were all showing as merchant-facing beliefs — but they describe your relationship with Jefe, not your business. Jefe still reads them (they're how it eases a markdown after you've called one too aggressive); it just no longer presents them to you as findings about your store. `app/lib/merchant-memory/deterministic-belief-registry.server.js`, `tests/belief-audience.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe now asks for cost prices without waiting for you to go looking.** The one thing Jefe genuinely cannot work out for itself is what your products cost you — and without it there's no margin, no profit, no "which of your bestsellers actually make money". The invitation to add it was written and correct, and it was created in exactly one place: the moment a merchant opened the Merchant Memory view. Anyone who only used the chat never had it generated, and the chat reads these from the database — so the most useful thing Jefe could say was conditional on visiting the page least likely to be visited. It's now refreshed on the existing coalesced background job that already runs after merchant messages, so it costs nothing extra and stays off the home's load path. A store that already has costs isn't nagged. `app/services/shopify-backfill-worker.server.js`, `app/lib/merchant-memory/conversation.server.js`, `tests/gap-questions-reach-the-chat.test.mjs`.

- **Six more of the things Jefe knows now read as sentences: your bestseller, how much of your money is sitting in stock, what's out of stock, how much you depend on your biggest customers, how long it's been since anything sold, and which products haven't sold in 90 days.** Statement coverage goes 12 → 18 of 114 merchant-facing beliefs. Each stays within what the belief actually supports: customer concentration is stated as exposure rather than scolded (a few big loyal accounts is a fine business, and Jefe can't see from here whether it's a risk), products with no sales are not called dead stock (that needs stock and cost data too — this one could be new or seasonal, and Jefe doesn't guess), and "nothing's sold" stays quiet until it's been a few days, because "0 days since your last order" is noise rather than insight. `app/lib/merchant-memory/belief-statement.server.js`, `tests/belief-statement-tranche.test.mjs`.

- **When Jefe can't do something for you, it now says so like a person and tells you why — instead of "this move is advisory until a typed action preview is available".** Jefe has one thing it can actually execute and can talk usefully about far more, so *most* recommendations landed on that sentence: our vocabulary, written as an absence, offering nothing to do. The most common outcome read as a failed action rather than as advice. It now reads as a position — "this one's yours to make" — and where Jefe knows the reason it gives it: a limit you set (named, because that's the thing you can change), or that it couldn't be confident enough to act alone. That reason already existed and was being thrown away: it was computed, written to the database, and never read back, because the query didn't select the column. Where there's no specific reason, Jefe says the general thing rather than inventing a cause. `app/lib/actions/action-resolution.server.js`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/components/app-home/data.ts`, `tests/instruct-path.test.mjs`.

- **⛔ The internal ops panel at admin.mynamejefe.com has no login — deliberately, for now.** Founder's call, reaffirmed 2026-08-13: leave it open for at least 2-3 weeks. The panel is cross-merchant by design and the URL is publicly reachable, so this is a known, accepted exposure rather than an oversight — revisit ~early September. `OPS_PASSWORD` is still set in Railway, so restoring is a code change only: delete `OPS_AUTH_DISABLED` and its branch in `apps/ops/server.mjs`. `apps/ops/server.mjs`.

- **Jefe's health check stops reporting the worker as dead while it's busy importing a store.** The worker stamps a liveness heartbeat at the start of each tick, but a tick drains several queued jobs one after another and then runs the whole maintenance chain — so once that took longer than the 90-second stale window, a perfectly healthy loop showed as `stale`. The heaviest drain there is happens to be a merchant onboarding, whose phases deliberately enqueue one another so importing doesn't feel stalled: the exact moment we most need to trust the signal was the moment it lied. Seen twice in production on 2026-08-12, both times with one job running. The heartbeat now also stamps after each completed job, so genuine progress keeps it fresh — while a loop that is truly wedged still goes stale, because nothing completes. `app/services/shopify-backfill-worker.server.js`, `app/lib/observability/heartbeat.server.js`, `tests/worker-heartbeat-during-drain.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe can now say seven more of the things it knows in plain English, instead of showing you the metric name.** Merchant Memory could phrase 5 of its 114 merchant-facing beliefs as sentences; the rest degraded to raw labels like `Order Value Mean To Median Ratio · Trailing 90d`. Seven more now read as Jefe talking: live £0 variants, negative stock, discount depth, single-item baskets, repeat-customer rate, your biggest month of the year, and the cost-price gap. Each stays exactly as strong as the belief behind it — zero-price variants are reported without being called a mistake (samples and add-ons are legitimately free), negative stock is named as a symptom rather than a fault, the repeat rate is stated and deliberately not graded (the same number means opposite things for a considered purchase and a habitual one), and the peak month says how many months of history it is drawn from. Missing cost data is phrased as an invitation with the exact place to enter it and what it unlocks, never a refusal. Also fixes a shared formatter trap where a belief whose percentage never computed rendered as a confident "0%" — `Number(null)` is `0` — so Jefe stated figures it did not have. `app/lib/merchant-memory/belief-statement.server.js`, `tests/belief-statement-tranche.test.mjs`.

- **A fresh chat no longer claims store-level work happened “in this conversation.”** Proposed actions and recent store updates now stay out of the blank-chat rail and are labelled as store updates when shown, so a new conversation can be genuinely empty. Jefe also drops a dead-stock markdown action intent when the Plan recommendation is really about customer re-engagement or retention, preventing a customer-looking title from becoming a proposed clearance move. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/lib/merchant-plan/schema.server.js`, `tests/memory-surface-wiring.test.mjs`, `tests/merchant-plan.test.mjs`.

- **After the first action is approved or tracked, Jefe now opens the app instead of waiting on the onboarding screen.** The action confirmation still appears briefly, but the handoff token now completes itself and loads the normal app home, so merchants do not have to notice and press the separate “Open the app” button after they have already made their first decision. `app/components/fast-value-onboarding.tsx`, `tests/fast-onboarding.test.mjs`, `tests/onboarding-flow.test.mjs`.

- **The home no longer shows two Settings gears.** Settings stays in the top-right app chrome next to "Open the app", while the duplicate gear beside the home date is gone so the chat header stays clean. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/routes/app.tsx`, `tests/surface-reachability.test.mjs`.

- **A quick reinstall can no longer crash the app before onboarding loads.** If a shop was uninstalled and immediately reinstalled while Jefe was healing tenant records, the retry path could find the old shop row but skip the same merchant-relinking guard used by normal requests. The next connector write then pointed at a missing merchant and `/app.data` returned a server error instead of letting the merchant back into Jefe. Reinstall recovery now relinks the shop before refreshing the Shopify connector account on both paths, with a regression test for the race. `app/lib/ingestion/shopify/tenant.server.js`, `tests/tenant-resilience.test.mjs`.

- **First-run onboarding no longer gets stuck after a fast read misses the first move.** The recent-order bootstrap can be stricter than the full business read; when it found no eligible contract, onboarding used to stop at Context even after the full read had produced a supported insight and recommendation. Jefe now keeps waiting while the full read is still active, and if the full read produces the first move, onboarding shows that Insight and Action instead of sending the merchant into Jefe empty. `app/lib/onboarding/fast-onboarding.server.js`, `app/components/fast-value-onboarding.tsx`, `tests/fast-onboarding.test.mjs`.

- **The onboarding screen no longer shows internal stage-jump controls.** In development mode, the first-run footer could expose an internal `CONNECTCONTEXTINSIGHTACTIONAPP` control that crowded the real learning status and looked broken. Those buttons are gone from the onboarding screen, so merchants and local testers see only the real setup flow. `app/components/fast-value-onboarding.tsx`, `app/styles/jefe.css`, `tests/onboarding-flow.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe now records who each thing it knows is *for*, so our own ingestion diagnostics can't leak into what a merchant reads.** Every belief carries an `audience`: `merchant` (Jefe may say it), `internal` (our ingestion health — orphan line items, link and timestamp coverage) or `model` (Jefe reasons with it but doesn't say it yet). Of 140 beliefs that is 114 / 19 / 7. Until now the function that decided merchant-visibility excluded only the 7 held-back ones, so all 19 diagnostics passed a gate whose name promised they wouldn't — and they stayed off the merchant's screen purely because the Memory view *also* filtered them by category. Two rules, free to drift, with the truth in neither; a second consumer of that gate, or a tidy-up of that filter, would have surfaced nineteen debug counters as things Jefe had worked out about the business. Now one rule. An unclassified belief defaults to `merchant`, so a new one shows up where someone will notice it rather than silently vanishing. No change to what a merchant sees today. `app/lib/merchant-memory/deterministic-belief-registry.server.js`, `app/components/merchant-memory-view.tsx`, `tests/belief-audience.test.mjs`.

- **Merchant Memory chat history is redacted before it reaches the AI.** The current message was already cleaned, but the helper that prepares earlier turns only shortened them; if a merchant had pasted a customer email or phone number earlier in the thread, that raw text could be included as context for the next answer. The thread prompt now uses the same redaction path before truncation, and the continuity test fixture matches the tenant fields the real conversation rows carry so CI guards the privacy path correctly. `app/lib/merchant-memory/conversation.server.js`, `tests/conversation-context-continuity.test.mjs`.

- **A message Jefe can't answer says so again, and the Try again button now actually retries.** The failure state landed earlier today and lost its visible half in the holistic-memory merge: the server still returned a failure and the route still accepted a retry, but the home rendered neither — so a failed message went back to showing nothing. The retry was also left pointing at the old memory-topic conversation while messages moved to the general-chat one, which would have reported "nothing to retry" while looking like it worked. Both re-fitted onto the new conversation path rather than reverted. The tests that let this through passed the whole time because they exercise the server functions; the new ones assert the *surface* renders a failure and a retry, and that retry targets the same conversation the composer posts into — verified to fail on exactly the shape that regressed (server intact, UI missing). `app/lib/merchant-memory/general-chat.server.js`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `tests/chat-retry-wiring.test.mjs`.

- **Merchants can open Merchant Memory again, and reach Settings at all.** Removing the "See everything Jefe knows" link from the home was deliberate — the chat-log home shouldn't carry it — but it was the *only* navigation off the home, and its replacement hadn't landed. So for a stretch this afternoon the product's core object rendered perfectly and could not be opened by anybody, and Settings went with it. Nothing errored and nothing looked wrong in the code, which is what made it survivable: the route still handled `?view=memory` throughout. A quiet gear in the home header now leads to Settings, and Merchant Memory is reached from there — not back on the home. Tests assert there is a way *in*, because route handling is not reachability and would have passed all the way through the outage. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/routes/app.settings.tsx`, `tests/surface-reachability.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe's read of how often customers come back now actually tells merchants apart.** Checked against 203 real merchants, the old bands put roughly three in five of them in the same bucket and left the slowest one empty — it looked like Jefe understood something, while saying nearly the same thing about everyone. The bands are now named for the rhythm a merchant would recognise — every fortnight, monthly, every few months, seasonal — and cut where businesses genuinely differ, so a coffee refill and a jeweller no longer read the same. `app/lib/merchant-memory/shopify-derivations.server.js`.

- **"That's right" and "forget that" can find what you meant again.** Alongside the thread fix above, the single stored pointer that records *which fact you were just discussing* had been quietly stuck empty. It was restored with a `??`, which treats a missing value as absent but an empty list as a real answer — and every turn that changes nothing writes an empty list, so the first one switched off the rebuild-from-history path for the rest of the conversation. It was empty on a real production conversation. This is the half the thread fix does not reach: sending the conversation to the AI helps it *read* a reference, but "that's right" and "forget that" are resolved by the non-AI path, which relies on this pointer — so a merchant confirming a fact Jefe named one message earlier was asked "which part should I mark as confirmed?". `app/lib/merchant-memory/conversation.server.js`, `tests/conversation-context-continuity.test.mjs` (which also pins the redaction of thread text crossing the AI boundary — the code did it, nothing asserted it).

### Added

- **Approving a Jefe suggestion now runs the right action, and Jefe tells merchants what kind of business it thinks they run.** Approving used to route every suggestion down the pricing path, so any other kind of suggestion would have been quietly refused with nothing to show for the tap. Approvals now go to the action they belong to. And the seven readings Jefe had been forming privately about a business — how it sells, how wide its range is, what customers spend, where it delivers, how often they come back, whose brands it stocks, and whether buying is a decision or a habit — now appear in Merchant Memory where they can be confirmed or corrected. `app/lib/actions/execute-approved-action.server.js`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/lib/merchant-memory/deterministic-belief-registry.server.js`.

- **Jefe can now propose filling in a merchant's missing product categories, and act on it once approved.** The last pieces of Listing copy: Jefe finds sellable products with no category, works out what to call each one from how the merchant already files everything else, and puts that up as a normal proposal with a reason per product ("your other Hawkstone products are Beer"). Approving it applies the change; it can be undone; and a product the merchant categorises themselves in the meantime is left exactly as they left it. `app/lib/actions/{action-resolution,wire-listing-copy-execution}.server.js`.

- **When Jefe suggests a product type, it uses the merchant's own words for it.** Jefe now works out what to call an uncategorised product by looking at how the merchant already categorises everything else — if their other products from a brand are all filed under "Beer", that is what it proposes, not what some external catalogue would call it. Each suggestion says why in a line the merchant can check ("your other Hawkstone products are Beer"). Where the evidence is genuinely mixed, Jefe proposes nothing and asks instead, rather than guessing and quietly filing something wrong. `app/lib/actions/{listing-copy-proposal,listing-copy-shopify-client}.server.js`, `tests/listing-copy-proposal.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe can fill in missing product types — built, and still switched off.** Settings shows "Listing copy" as coming soon; this is the first real piece of it. Jefe can now set the product type on sellable products that have none, which is the field search, reporting and Jefe's own read of a merchant's range all depend on. It only ever fills a blank: a product the merchant has already categorised is left alone, including if they categorise it while Jefe is mid-run. Every change can be undone, and undo keeps working even if the feature is switched back off. Nothing is live — the setting stays on "soon" until the flag is turned on deliberately. Descriptions and titles are deliberately not included yet; those are the merchant's voice, not a taxonomy field. `app/lib/actions/{listing-copy-adapter,action-intent}.server.js`, `tests/listing-copy-adapter.test.mjs`.

- **What Jefe says can now be measured instead of eyeballed.** A harness that replays multi-turn merchant conversations through the real chat path — the same function the home composer calls, no mocks — against a locally seeded store whose beliefs come from the real derivation pipeline, and grades the replies deterministically: internal reasoning leaking into copy, questions left unanswered, context dropped between turns, machine vocabulary. Multi-turn, because the worst failures only appear on the third message. And **two deliberately unlike businesses** (an online-only skincare brand, a garden centre running a POS till), because generic advice is only visible by comparison — those scenarios grade the *pair*. That is what it caught first: asked the same margin question, both stores got a byte-identical reply, 100% similarity between a business with cost prices on everything and one with almost none. Reports diff against a saved baseline, so a prompt change arrives with a number attached instead of an opinion. Recorded baseline before today's fixes: 57 broken / 12 poor across 22 turns. `apps/shopify/scripts/answer-quality/` (README there; local database enforced, fixtures invented).

### Fixed
- **Jefe no longer treats selling on Amazon as if it were selling from your own website.** Jefe only recognised three ways of selling — your own site, in person, and invoiced orders — and quietly binned everything else together. Checked against 207 real merchants, that discarded marketplaces (Amazon, eBay, Faire), social shops (TikTok, Facebook), custom storefronts and Shopify's own wholesale channel. Selling through someone else's marketplace is a different business: you don't own the storefront, the customer, or the pricing, so advice that assumes you do is wrong. Jefe now tells them apart, and records a smaller marketplace presence even when it isn't the main way a merchant sells. `app/lib/merchant-memory/shopify-derivations.server.js`.

- **Jefe now remembers what you just said to it on the home chat.** Every turn was answered as though it were the first: the conversation was read from the database and then thrown away, because only one belief key survived into the prompt the model saw. So "for what you said before, the cost-per-item in Shopify" had no "before" to resolve against, and Jefe asked the merchant to re-explain what they had explained one message earlier — the failure the founder hit hardest. The last eight turns now go to the interpreter, oldest first, redacted like every other merchant text that crosses the AI boundary, and the model is told not to ask for anything already said. A second defect sat underneath it: the home path read the thread with `orderBy: asc` **and** a row limit, which returns the twelve *oldest* messages — so on any conversation longer than twelve turns, "recent history" was ancient history. It now reads the tail, the way the action chat always has. `app/lib/merchant-memory/conversation.server.js`, `tests/conversation-thread-memory.test.mjs`.

- **One of Jefe's readings of how often customers come back could never happen.** The slowest band was set at more than six months between orders — checked against 203 real merchants' actual repeat behaviour, nothing ever reaches it, so the band existed but never described anyone. It now sits where the real slow tail actually is. `app/lib/merchant-memory/shopify-derivations.server.js`.

- **What Jefe knows about a business now actually reaches Jefe when it answers.** Jefe can only fit about forty of the things it knows into any one answer, and it picks them by matching the merchant's words — so its read of the *kind* of business, which by design matches nobody's words, lost every place to whatever was topically closer. It was being worked out and then never used. Jefe now always carries the shape of the business into an answer, so "what should I reorder?" is answered differently for a one-product maker than for a five-thousand-line retailer — without ever crowding out the thing actually being discussed. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{conversation,deterministic-belief-registry}.server.js`.

- **A slow or rate-limited Groq response can no longer leave Jefe thinking forever instead of trying Gemini.** Jefe already had a Gemini fallback, but two waits could keep it out of reach: the Groq deadline ended as soon as response headers arrived, so a stalled response body could hang indefinitely, and a rate limit's uncapped `Retry-After` could pause the primary provider for minutes. The deadline now covers the complete response and Groq's one local retry stays short, so either failure reaches the production `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` fallback promptly. `app/lib/llm/providers/groq.server.js`, `tests/llm-provider-fallback.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe's backup model can no longer quietly rot into a dead one.** When Groq (the primary) is slow or rate-limited, Jefe falls back to a Gemini flash-lite model — but that fallback was pinned to a specific version, and specific versions get retired: `gemini-2.0-flash-lite` and `gemini-2.5-flash-lite` both now return "no longer available", which would leave the safety net dead exactly when the primary needs it. The fallback default (and the voice-note transcriber that shares it) now point at `gemini-flash-lite-latest`, an alias Google keeps current, so it can't decay to a dead model name — verified callable for both text and audio. The default request timeout also moves from 8s to 30s to match the real ~6k-token conversation prompt, so an environment that doesn't override it isn't failing ~40% of turns out of the box. `app/lib/llm/config.server.js`, `tests/voice-feedback.test.mjs`.

- **A message Jefe can't answer no longer replaces the conversation with a setup screen, and can now be retried without saying it twice.** The merchant's message is stored before Jefe is asked to reply, so anything that failed after that point threw out of the request and the route's error boundary took over the page — a merchant mid-conversation was told "Jefe is still getting set up", with their thread gone from the screen. The home also rendered no error for *any* failure, so every other way a reply could fail simply dropped the merchant's text and left nothing behind. And there was no retry at all: the only way to ask again was to retype, which would have stored the same message a second time. Jefe now returns that failure instead of throwing, says so inside the thread in its own voice, and offers a Try again that answers the message already sitting there. The prompt to retry is read from the conversation rather than the failed request, so it survives a reload, a new tab, and the error boundary's own "Try again" — the merchant is never left holding an unanswered message with no way forward. Note this is the *failure* path only: an LLM timeout is already caught a layer below and answered by the deterministic interpreter, which is a separate answer-quality problem. `app/lib/merchant-memory/conversation.server.js`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `tests/daily-chat-retry.test.mjs`.

- **A slow Groq response can no longer leave Jefe thinking forever instead of trying Gemini.** Jefe already fell back to Gemini when Groq returned a rate limit, timeout or temporary provider error, but the Groq deadline ended as soon as response headers arrived. If Groq opened a response and then stalled while sending its body, the first-read job waited indefinitely and the fallback never got a turn. The deadline now covers the complete response, so a stalled body times out and follows the existing retry and Gemini fallback path. `app/lib/llm/providers/groq.server.js`, `tests/llm-provider-fallback.test.mjs`.

- **A healthy first read no longer stalls while its Merchant Memory is being saved.** The fast onboarding read groups its small set of safe beliefs under one database lock so the simultaneous full-history rebuild cannot publish conflicting versions. Prisma’s five-second default transaction limit was too short for those belief, history and evidence writes over the production database, so the transaction closed mid-write and every retry failed at the same point. That guarded transaction now has an explicit bounded production-safe wait and runtime, allowing the evidence-backed insight to reach the merchant while preserving the same lock and atomic write guarantees. `app/lib/merchant-memory/service.server.js`, `tests/fast-onboarding.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe now answers the merchant in its own voice, instead of reading out its private notes.** When Jefe wasn't sure what a merchant meant — "give me the Shopify URL for where this is" — it replied with the reasoning it writes for itself: "The merchant asked for a Shopify URL but did not specify which resource… Need clarification on what 'this' refers to." Third person, written about the merchant rather than to them — and then it bolted an unrelated question about product costs onto the end of replies that had nothing to do with costs. Jefe now keeps that private reasoning private and asks a real, second-person question back ("Which page do you mean — a product, an order, or a collection?"), and a reply that changes nothing no longer tacks a stray question on. The nudge to add cost prices, where it still belongs, now reads as a person wrote it rather than "cost prices for only 0% of your products". `app/lib/merchant-memory/conversation.server.js`, `app/lib/llm/structured-operation-schema.server.js`, `tests/conversational-merchant-memory.test.mjs`.

### Added

- **New merchants now reach one useful, evidence-backed move without waiting for their full Shopify history.** Connection starts an independent fast Merchant Memory read alongside the existing full backfill, asks one lightweight priority question, and carries one supported insight through to a real executable or tracked recommendation. The new `CONNECT -> CONTEXT -> INSIGHT -> ACTION -> APP` experience never exposes import counts or technical queue progress; its one-time APP handoff shows only work Jefe is genuinely tracking, then later visits open Daily Home normally. Limited evidence is kept honest through explicit evidence contracts, observed-window provenance and confidence caps; long-history claims such as dead stock, repeat behaviour and seasonality are excluded. Track-only recommendations receive a scheduled outcome review, while executable recommendations still use the existing typed, idempotent, reversible action path. Bootstrap and full-history health are independently observable and recoverable. `app/lib/onboarding/`, `app/components/fast-value-onboarding.tsx`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/services/shopify-backfill-{status,worker}.server.js`, `prisma/schema.prisma`, `app/styles/jefe.css`.

### Security

- **A second kind of action can no longer be switched on by accident, or run down the price-change path.** Jefe's action layer was built to govern many kinds of action but only ever had one — marking prices down — and three places quietly assumed that. Whether an action was allowed to touch a store read the _price-markdown_ switch rather than its own, so the moment a second kind of action existed it would have been treated as switched on, because the price-markdown switch is on in production. The safety limit on how much a single run may change ("no more than N products, no deeper than X%") silently passed anything it couldn't measure, and a different kind of action is precisely the thing it can't measure. And the code that carries out an approved price change never checked what kind of action it had been handed. Nothing was ever miswritten to a merchant's store — the last of those stopped at a database constraint — but it stopped there by accident rather than by design. Each of the three now refuses by default: an action reads its own switch, an unmeasurable run is blocked rather than allowed, and the price-change path rejects anything that isn't a price change. `app/lib/actions/{action-intent,action-resolution,clearance-adapter,wire-clearance-execution}.server.js`, `app/lib/merchant-plan/service.server.js`, +12 tests.

### Added

- **Jefe can now tell a maker from a retailer.** Product type and vendor were already being collected but only ever used to rank which lines sold best — nothing read them to work out what sort of range a shop actually keeps. Jefe now distinguishes someone selling their own label in one category from a shop stocking five other people's brands across eight, and the specialist stocking many brands from the general retailer. Those are opposite businesses that can turn identical revenue, and they need opposite advice about range, stock and clearance. Where a merchant has not filled in product types or vendors, Jefe says nothing rather than guessing. Not shown to merchants yet. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{shopify-derivations,deterministic-belief-registry}.server.js`, `tests/business-shape-beliefs.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe's AI can now fall back to more than just Gemini — Kimi K3 and other OpenAI-compatible models can serve as backups (built, not yet switched on).** The provider layer only knew Groq and Gemini, so a bad moment for both would leave Jefe with no model at all. It now has one generic OpenAI-compatible adapter behind a small registry, so a model like Kimi K3 (Moonshot) becomes a usable backup by setting a key and pointing the fallback at it — no new code — and adding another vendor (e.g. Meta Spark) is a one-line registry entry. **Dark until keyed:** an un-keyed provider yields nothing and changes nothing, and the live Groq→Gemini path is untouched. Verified against Kimi's published OpenAI-compatible contract (`json_schema` structured output, Bearer auth) with mocked-fetch tests; turning it on needs the provider's API key in the environment (and Meta Spark's base URL confirmed). `app/lib/llm/providers/openai-compatible.server.js`, `app/lib/llm/{config,provider}.server.js`, `tests/openai-compatible-provider.test.mjs`, `.env.example`.

- **Jefe now reads three more things about the shape of a business: what customers spend, where it delivers, and whether buying is a decision or a habit.** Spend is described as the merchant's own spread — what a typical order is, what the quiet and busy ends look like, and whether a few unusually large orders sit inside an otherwise ordinary order book, which usually means trade buyers. Delivery is described by concentration: one market, one market plus some export, or genuinely international. And Jefe distinguishes a business people consider carefully and rarely repeat from one they buy from out of habit — the difference between "win them back" and "there is no back". Like the first three, these are Jefe's own read and are **not shown to merchants yet**. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{shopify-derivations,deterministic-belief-registry}.server.js`, `tests/business-shape-beliefs.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe is starting to tell one kind of business from another.** Until now Jefe's understanding of a store held its name, currency, order counts and activity — nothing about what it actually sells or how. A lipstick brand and a car dealership looked identical to it, differing only in the numbers, so advice came out generic no matter how well it was written. Jefe now works out three things about the shape of a business from data it already holds: whether it sells online, in a shop, or to trade; how wide its range is; and how often customers come back. These are Jefe's own read, not confirmed fact, and they are **not shown to merchants yet** — they run against real stores first so we can check what Jefe concludes before it tells anyone what kind of business they run. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{shopify-derivations,deterministic-belief-registry}.server.js`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `tests/business-shape-beliefs.test.mjs`.

- **Jefe now remembers the thread of the business across chats, without confusing old discussion for current truth.** A merchant can start a new chat on Home, continue from Slack or email, refer back to an earlier recommendation, or ask what was discussed before. Jefe searches the same bounded, shop-isolated memory each time, gives current merchant-confirmed facts priority, labels earlier positions as historical, and keeps replies attached to the message that produced them so simultaneous channel replies cannot cross. Home now has a compact chat history and a clear New chat action while keeping the active conversation dominant.

- **Ordinary merchant conversation can now teach Jefe durable operating rules.** Merchant-authored turns are checked for registered facts, priorities and constraints after they are saved; deterministic validation—not the model—decides whether anything becomes durable Memory. Clear corrections replace current truth without erasing history, ambiguous contradictions become questions, generated replies cannot teach themselves, and asking Jefe to forget something keeps it out of later summaries and rebuilds. The first registered durable policy covers rejecting blanket storewide discounts, so future Plans can respect that instruction immediately.

- **Conversation recall now has rebuildable search, diagnostics and safe fallbacks.** Canonical messages stay in the database while sanitised full-text documents, optional 768-dimension embeddings and structured summaries make cross-chat recall fast. Failed or disabled embeddings fall back to lexical search, indexing and recent failures appear in health checks, existing messages backfill without retroactively creating beliefs, and Shopify privacy erasure now covers conversations, search documents, learning candidates, diagnostics and action ledgers.

- **Jefe can now be taught a second kind of action without rewriting how actions work.** Everything about carrying out an action — how far it's allowed to go, how to judge whether it's safe to do unsupervised, and how to describe it to the merchant — was written as though marking prices down were the only thing Jefe would ever do. Each kind of action now states those things for itself, and Jefe refuses to run one that hasn't. The visible payoff is that Jefe can no longer describe one kind of action in another's words: an action it doesn't yet know how to explain is left unsaid rather than announced as a price cut, and the same is true in the history of what it has done. It also fixes a smaller inconsistency merchants could actually hit — a clearance quoted one figure for the cash it would free when first suggested and a different one after a page reload, because the first counted products the safety floor had already excluded. `app/lib/actions/action-resolution.server.js`, +4 tests.

- **Jefe now records why it couldn't do something autonomously, so it can tell the merchant instead of going quiet.** A merchant who turns an action autonomous should get Jefe doing what it safely can and _raising what it can't_, with the steps to unblock it. Jefe already worked out the answer three different ways — is the action reversible, is it within the safety limits, does it exceed a cap the merchant set — and then threw two of the three away as soon as it had them, keeping only a log line. Those reasons are now kept with the action, so the explanation a merchant gets can be the real one rather than a shrug. `app/lib/actions/action-resolution.server.js`, +5 tests.

- **Every kind of action now has to say who it suits and what success looks like, before it can exist.** Two gaps that would have compounded with each new action: nothing recorded _which businesses_ an action makes sense for — a clearance markdown is sensible for a lipstick brand and absurd for a car dealer — and nothing recorded how to tell afterwards whether it worked, so only price markdowns could ever be judged. Both are now part of the definition of an action rather than something bolted on later, and both are described in terms Jefe can actually observe about a business instead of an industry label. Scoring a business against those terms comes next; this is the slot and the shared vocabulary. `app/lib/actions/action-intent.server.js`, +7 tests.

### Changed

- **A new Home chat now looks and behaves like a fresh conversation.** The selected thread is explicitly labelled as the current chat, empty chats have an honest blank state, duplicate untitled chats are disambiguated in history, and the transcript contains only messages that belong to that conversation. Live inventory signals, recent action reports and Jefe's proposed next move remain available in a separate Store updates popover instead of appearing as unexplained messages in every new thread. When a message is sent, its text leaves the composer immediately and appears in the transcript; the disabled button stays labelled Send while Jefe replies instead of changing to Thinking. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `tests/memory-surface-wiring.test.mjs`.

- **Revenue totals are correct again for stores whose customers pay in different currencies.** Jefe briefly split these stores' revenue up by the currency each customer paid in, and told them there was no single total. Both were wrong: the amounts Jefe stores are always already converted to the store's own currency, so they add up normally — the currency on an order only records what the shopper paid in. A store selling to Europe now sees one correct revenue figure in its own currency, matching what Jefe's understanding of the business already said, instead of a split-up view that labelled pounds as euros. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{commerce-calculations,commerce-analyst}.server.js`.

### Added

- **Jefe can now load a real merchant's trading history into an isolated simulation.** The Quiver corpus loader turns mapped warehouse orders into the same commerce records a connected store produces, so Merchant Memory, insights, goals and plan all run against a real business without a single change to how they work. Simulated stores are kept physically apart from real ones — their own database, a store address that cannot resolve, and no store connection at all, so nothing Jefe does in simulation can reach anyone's shop. Re-loading the same merchant updates rather than duplicates, and orders whose money does not make sense are held back and counted rather than quietly averaged into what Jefe believes. `tools/quiver-corpus/src/load.mjs`.

- **Merchants can now tell Jefe to forget something, just by saying so.** Confirming, correcting and answering already worked by typing in the Merchant Memory composer; forgetting did not, so a merchant who saw something Jefe had wrong about their business had no way to remove it — only to argue with it. Saying "forget that" or "that's not true any more" now retires the understanding. Because this is the one thing a merchant can say that _destroys_ something, it is deliberately cautious: Jefe always shows which understanding it is about to drop, with its current value, and waits for a yes; if it cannot tell which one is meant, it asks instead of guessing; and "don't forget we close on Mondays" is read as the merchant teaching Jefe something, not erasing it. Facts read straight from Shopify cannot be forgotten — the store still says otherwise — so Jefe offers to correct those instead. A forget can be undone by saying "undo that". `app/lib/merchant-memory/{conversation,service,constants,conversational-belief-registry}.server.js`, `app/lib/llm/structured-operation-schema.server.js`, `tests/conversation-obsolete-op.test.mjs`.

- **Quiver's warehouse is now verified as a usable input, and its bad data is flagged rather than trusted.** Checked the corpus mapping against the live Redshift warehouse: 247 merchants and 21.6M orders reaching back to 2021 and current to yesterday, with the column contract matching exactly and a real order mapped end-to-end. Two findings changed the design — orders arrive in six currencies (an order can be entirely non-GBP, so nothing may assume sterling), and 43,873 orders in the last year carry a discount larger than their own subtotal, three of them over £100,000. Jefe now flags implausible order money instead of quietly averaging it into a belief, and records what it flagged so a reviewer can see what was set aside rather than the data looking cleaner than it is. `tools/quiver-corpus/src/{map,quiver-schema}.mjs`.

- **Real merchants can now be replayed through Jefe as simulated stores.** Added `tools/quiver-corpus`, which maps orders from Quiver's Redshift warehouse into Jefe's canonical commerce records so the existing pipeline — deterministic beliefs, evidence, insights, goals, plan and action chat — can be run against real businesses and inspected in Ops, without a parallel pipeline or a bespoke parser. Corpus stores are structurally isolated from real tenants: they use a `quiver_sim` platform and an unresolvable `*.corpus.invalid` domain, so they carry no Shopify session or token and the action layer cannot write to any store from one. Customer emails are pseudonymised with a required salt, personal columns are off unless explicitly enabled, and Quiver's full stringified platform payload is never carried. The mapping layer and its write guards are built and covered by 18 tests; the data pull itself is still waiting on Metabase access, and the gaps in what Quiver can tell us — no stock, no cost, no per-line prices — are stamped onto every corpus store so a belief is never built on absence. `tools/quiver-corpus/`, `MANIFEST.json`, `.worktreeinclude`.

- **Connecting Slack now lands on a working Channels panel — with the "connected" confirmation — instead of a placeholder.** The Channels + Integrations panels are now mounted in the (merchant-reachable) settings surface, and the `?channelNotice=` the Slack callbacks pass — previously orphaned, so the confirmation vanished — now renders. Per the founder, the sub-nav opens **Autonomy-first** (Autonomy · Integrations · Channels · Notifications) and the account panel is renamed **"Notifications"** (keeps the `settings` slug). `app/routes/app.settings.tsx`.

- **Settings surface — Slack Channels panel + Integrations panel (components), behind the unlinked settings route.** Building out the founder's Integrations / Channels / Settings left-nav (2026-08-12): the **Channels panel** restores Slack as a first-class settings destination — connect via an OAuth popup (reusing the existing `channels.slack.start` / `channels.slack.callback` routes), choose the channel Jefe posts in (it sends a confirming hello), and disconnect — deliberately **Slack-first** ("get Slack going, nail one"); Teams / iMessage / WhatsApp are deferred. The **Integrations panel** renders Jefe's detected tool-stack but shows only **surfaceable** (≥0.7-confidence, verification-gated) detections, framed as inference ("we think you're running X — spotted via …") with an honestly-gated "Coming soon" connect (never a fake button), and an honest "still learning your stack" empty state — which is what every current merchant sees (0 verified detections). Both are data-in components that mount into `app/routes/app.settings.tsx` once the shell session wires them. A new `POST /api/channels/slack` resource route owns the post-connect ops (refresh channels / save destination / disconnect), and the Slack OAuth callback now lands on `/app/settings?panel=channels` instead of the removed onboarding `"channels"` step — fixing a merchant dead-end where connecting Slack bounced back to Connect with no confirmation. Nothing is merchant-reachable yet: the settings surface isn't linked from the home (the entry point is the founder's call) and the panels await wiring. `app/components/settings/{ChannelsPanel,IntegrationsPanel}.tsx`, `app/routes/api.channels.slack.tsx`, `app/routes/channels.slack.callback.tsx`.

### Fixed

- **LLM fallback attempts are no longer hidden behind a single misleading Groq error.** The provider correctly attempted Gemini after a fallback-eligible Groq failure, but if Gemini also failed the usage ledger wrote only one error under the primary model and discarded the fallback identity; operators therefore saw “Groq 429” with no evidence that Gemini had run. Successful fallback now records the failed primary attempt plus the provider/model that answered, while dual failure records both attempts and logs the fallback's sanitized status and reason code. Prompts and responses remain unlogged. `app/lib/llm/provider.server.js`, `tests/llm-provider-fallback.test.mjs`.

- **Tests can no longer spend production-style LLM quota or leak generation work to a running dev worker.** The test process already disabled its own backfill loop, but DB-backed tests used the same `jefe_dev` database as the live local app; that app could claim a test's queued Insights/Goals jobs and call Groq or Gemini in the background. The canonical runner now migrates and uses a deterministic, workspace-specific local test database, disables generation and embeddings, and runs files serially there. Remote builds may use only an explicitly test-named `TEST_DATABASE_URL`; a production-shaped `DATABASE_URL` is never reused for tests. A transport-level guard also blocks real Groq, Gemini, embedding and voice-transcription network clients under Node's test runner—even when a test is launched directly—while allowing only explicitly injected mocks. `package.json`, `scripts/run-tests.mjs`, `app/lib/llm/{config,external-call-guard}.server.js`, `app/lib/llm/providers/{groq,gemini}.server.js`, `app/lib/llm/transcribe-voice.server.js`, `app/lib/merchant-memory/embedding.server.js`, `tests/llm-test-isolation.test.mjs`.

- **A Groq rate limit now hands the request to Gemini immediately.** Groq returned repeated 429 responses with zero tokens processed, but Jefe treated the provider's `Retry-After` as a reason to keep Gemini waiting and could send another request that was guaranteed to fail. The first 429 now goes straight to the configured `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` fallback without sleeping or retrying Groq. Groq's advertised token window is retained locally, so further requests skip the exhausted provider until its reset instead of creating more 429 rows. Calls remain serialised per API key, and rate-limit logs contain only provider reason codes, counts and timings. `app/lib/llm/{config,errors}.server.js`, `app/lib/llm/providers/groq.server.js`, `app/lib/merchant-memory/passive-memory.server.js`, `.env.example`, `tests/llm-provider-fallback.test.mjs`.

- **Chat no longer mistakes an oversized local prompt guard for a Groq outage.** The app chat and merchant-message decision prompts could exceed Jefe's own 6,000-token input guard before any provider call, so the usage table showed a Groq error with no Gemini row even though no Groq or Gemini request had been sent. Chat generation now uses the intended 8,000-token hard cap, the decision prompt sends compact current-memory context, and Merchant Context packet assembly uses a conservative JSON-heavy estimate so Gemini fallback stays under the live limit. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{general-chat,merchant-context,passive-memory}.server.js`, `tests/holistic-merchant-memory.test.mjs`.

- **Chat now asks the model what a merchant message means before choosing what to do with it.** Mentioning “margin” inside an operating instruction previously triggered live commerce analysis because a broad keyword check mistook subject matter for intent. A dedicated structured LLM decision now chooses whether to save and acknowledge durable Memory, run commerce analysis, recall an earlier discussion, or answer normally; registered candidates still pass deterministic type, tenant, PII, scope and precedence validation before Jefe can say they were saved. The registry now has a typed margin-cost-basis policy for an explicit Shopify Cost per item instruction. Decision actions and reason codes are recorded without message text, explicit rules receive a truthful confirmation, cross-chat fallback excludes the just-sent message and uses a relevant earlier episode, and structured beliefs such as goals render as merchant-facing text rather than raw JSON. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{general-chat,passive-memory,commerce-analyst,conversational-belief-registry}.server.js`, `app/lib/merchant-memory/retrievers/semantic-memory-retriever.server.js`, `tests/{holistic-merchant-memory,commerce-analyst}.test.mjs`.

- **Insights onboarding no longer keeps its own generation job permanently queued.** The loading screen polls while Jefe is preparing findings, but each poll was treating the already-queued run as new work: it reset the shared job's attempts and moved its eligible time forward just as the worker tried to claim it. The skeleton could therefore remain forever even though Memory was ready and no model call had failed. Ordinary polling now reuses queued work unchanged; only the merchant's explicit Retry action can reset it. `app/lib/merchant-insights/service.server.js`, `tests/merchant-insights.test.mjs`.

- **A stalled Groq response can no longer hold the background worker forever.** The request timeout was cleared when HTTP headers arrived, before the response body had finished, so a provider that opened the response and then stopped sending could leave Insights—and every job behind it—running indefinitely. The abort deadline now covers body consumption and JSON parsing as well as the initial connection, allowing the existing retry/fallback path to recover. `app/lib/llm/providers/groq.server.js`, `tests/llm-provider-fallback.test.mjs`.

- **Opening Jefe from Shopify no longer waits on avoidable serial reads.** A production iframe load returned `200` from Railway but took almost seven seconds before the app document was ready, leaving Shopify showing its frame-level broken-page icon rather than Jefe's UI. The returning-merchant home loader now starts the independent memory, metrics, action, channel, chat, changelog and notification reads together, only waiting where a read genuinely needs another result such as the store currency. Slow `/app` loads also emit a structured `app-index-loader` warning with the duration and mode, so the next report is visible in app logs instead of only as a Railway HTTP total. `app/routes/app._index.tsx`.

- **Jefe now understands the money of merchants who sell abroad.** If a store took orders in more than one currency, Jefe refused to state anything about its money at all — average order value, revenue per trading day, stock value, refunds — and said the data quality was to blame. It wasn't: Shopify already converts every amount into the store's own currency before Jefe sees it, so the figures were always there to be worked out. Worse, a refused understanding isn't shown anywhere, so an international seller opened Merchant Memory to a blank where their money should be, with nothing to explain why. Jefe now states those figures in the store's own currency, and says which currency that is. `app/lib/merchant-memory/shopify-derivations.server.js`.

- **Money figures no longer contradict the currency Jefe says your store trades in.** Jefe worked out "your primary currency" from one set of records and stamped a currency onto each money figure from a slightly different set. On a store with a fairly even spread of currencies the two disagreed — Jefe could tell a UK merchant their primary currency was GBP and, on the same screen, that their average order was €100. Both now come from exactly the same records, so they cannot diverge. `app/lib/merchant-memory/shopify-derivations.server.js`, `tests/multi-currency-money-beliefs.test.mjs`.

- **Action chat no longer invents a money total for multi-currency merchants — it says it can't, the way memory already does.** For a store trading in more than one currency (about half of real merchants), the commerce analyst was summing GBP + EUR + AED into one unlabelled number and presenting it as an answer — precisely the question the Merchant Memory belief layer honestly refuses (`blocked_by_data_quality`). Now the analyst matches that bar: a money measure with no single currency is refused, never rendered as a bare cross-currency figure, and the bogus total is stripped at source so nothing downstream can quote it. Split-by-currency (per-currency rows) is the next step. Found + sized against Quiver's warehouse (~51% of real merchants are multi-currency). `commerce-calculations.server.js` (finalizeResult + stripMoneyTotals), `commerce-analyst.server.js` (calculationLines), +1 test.

- **The Merchant Memory page is shorter, more composed, and easy to talk to.** Matt's read of the first pass: useful, but "quite long", and it should be "easier to interact with" — it read as a read-only data dump. So `?view=memory` now opens with a prominent "Talk to Jefe" composer (with real example prompts) as the obvious thing to do, and what Jefe believes is grouped by **provenance** — "What you've told me" vs "What Jefe's worked out" — rather than by category, because a merchant trusts and corrects those differently (and the set is about to span a third source: beliefs pulled from connected tools like Meta/Google Ads). Each group is tight; the worked-out list is capped, most-worth-checking first (`confirmPriority`), with a "show all". Correction still commits only through the composer — no per-belief action controls — per the composer-only rule. `app/components/merchant-memory-view.tsx`, `tests/merchant-memory-view-composer.test.mjs`.

- **"Undo that" now actually undoes something.** Jefe recognised the request, replied as though it had acted, and changed nothing — the undo was written but never connected to the conversation, so every "undo that" was acknowledged and dropped. That mattered most straight after a merchant asked Jefe to forget something, where the reply explicitly offers the undo. Saying "undo that" now restores the last thing you changed, and says which one it put back. It is also scoped to the shop you're in, so it can't reach into another of your shops for something to reverse. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{conversation,service}.server.js`.

- **Telling Jefe to forget something now actually sticks.** A forgotten understanding was dropped from view but left in a state Jefe's nightly rebuild could not see, so the next rebuild worked the same fact out again and quietly put it back — the merchant's instruction undone without a word, days later. Corrections had always survived this; forgetting had not. A merchant's decision to forget something now outranks anything Jefe can infer, in both places that rebuild memory, and it is reversible: it is recorded as the merchant's own change, so "undo that" can put it back. `app/lib/merchant-memory/{service,store-understanding,constants}.server.js`.

- **Merchant Memory answers now stay inside the shop the merchant is asking about.** When a merchant runs more than one shop under the same account, the context Jefe assembles to answer a question inside a move could quietly include the other shop's beliefs, goals, insights, plan recommendations and action history — Jefe would have answered about the wrong shop with no sign anything was wrong. Every read is now scoped to one shop, and a request that arrives without a shop now reads nothing and records why, instead of reading everything. Shop-specific and account-wide beliefs are both still included, so nothing a merchant told Jefe about their business as a whole is lost. `app/lib/merchant-memory/context-retriever.server.js`, `tests/merchant-context-retriever.test.mjs`.

- **Connecting Slack no longer dead-ends the merchant back on Connect.** Beyond the popup callback (fixed alongside the Channels panel), `app._index` held a second Slack OAuth callback path — the loader block handling a `?channelProvider=slack&code=…` return — plus the `channel.*` action handlers (Slack test/select destination, WhatsApp verify/confirm), all still redirecting to the onboarding `"channels"` step that PR #68 removed. `resolveOnboardingStep` no longer recognises it, so each silently dropped the merchant back on Connect: a successful Slack connect showed no confirmation and errors were swallowed the same way. All six now redirect to `/app/settings?panel=channels` (a real route — chat 11's settings shell) via a shared `settingsChannelsPath` helper that preserves the App Bridge params (shop/host/embedded) and carries the notice. The dead onboarding channels UI (`ChannelsStep` / `channelProviderUrl`) is left for the re-home cleanup, not deleted. `app/routes/app._index.tsx`.

- **The Merchant Memory page renders again — and now tells a merchant what it's for.** Two fixes to the reachable `?view=memory` surface. **(1) It was blank in production:** `MerchantMemoryView` was a `lazy()` import inside `<Suspense fallback={null}>`, so during the chunk-load window — or when a stray App Bridge parent update trips React #421 and discards the boundary — the null fallback rendered nothing, silently (Matt clicked "See everything Jefe knows" and got a blank page). It's now a direct static import rendered without Suspense, mirroring `DailyHome`'s `8d753b8`, with a guard test that asserts the render is direct — the earlier reachability test proved the route was _reached_, not that it _renders_, and a lazy+null boundary satisfies "reached" while blank. **(2) It now leads with the ask, not the archive:** Matt asked twice what the page is _for_. The purpose is legible in the first line — check Jefe's got your business right, since every recommendation is built from these beliefs — and the top of the page is the few highest-`confirmPriority` beliefs framed as questions ("… — is that right?") plus the open questions, with the composer as the one answer box; the full labelled list sits below as browsable reference. The composer now also invites forget (the interpreter's obsolete op is live): "tell me to forget something — I'll show you what I'm about to drop and check first, and you can undo it." `app/components/merchant-memory-view.tsx`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `tests/merchant-memory-view-composer.test.mjs`.

### Changed

- **Onboarding no longer repeats Jefe’s app bar beneath Shopify’s own header.** The embedded Shopify header already identifies the app, so the second dark Jefe wordmark-and-menu row only consumed space and made the shell look duplicated. The onboarding progress and current task now begin directly beneath Shopify’s header. `app/components/fast-value-onboarding.tsx`, `app/styles/jefe.css`.

- **The home is now one conversation with Jefe — the "just a chat log" redesign (Shape B).** The returning-merchant home was a dashboard of cards — a hero "next move", Also-in-progress, Action history, What-I'm-watching, Where-we're-heading — which read as busy on a working store and as empty filler on a quiet one (the founder's call: "really bad" on a quiet day). It's now a single store-level conversation. Three things share that one thread: the real back-and-forth (the persistent composer posts `chat.message` into `getDailyChatThread`), Jefe reporting back on moves already made (each executed / declined action rendered as a message, from the same honest fields the old Action-history card used — never invented), and the current move as the freshest message, which zooms into its own action chat ("Talk this through →" → `?actionChat=<id>`). Two zoom levels, one conversation, no thread picker: the home composer posts `chat.message`, the per-move chat posts `action.chat.message`, and approve / decline / revise stay in the focused action chat — never a casual click on the home feed. On a genuinely quiet day the thread is a single grounded line, drawn from what Jefe is actually watching (or its most recent insight), never fabricated — so a quiet home reads as calm, not broken. Watching, Goals, autonomy and the changelog rail have left the home for their own surfaces; nothing gets added back. This also completes the fixed-instant date work the timezone fix flagged: `formatShortDate` now pins the store's own zone via `formatDateInZone`. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`.

- **The conversation home now shows what Jefe's watching and what it's working towards — and drops a dead fabricated checklist.** Three follow-ons to Shape B: (1) **run-out / refund heads-ups post into the thread** — Jefe proactively says e.g. "at how fast Rosehip Serum is selling, you'll run out around 4 Aug" from the Horizon lane's `getHorizonHeadsUps` (read-only, real stock/refund signals, `[]` on any error, never fabricated), so a quiet day shows Jefe genuinely watching something rather than a bare "nothing needs you". This is the first customer of the proactive-message pattern: re-rendered from state each load (not stored), shown below the primary move as a **feed** — **uncapped in the render** (the founder's call after live use — the chat is a notification feed; the realness bar keeps the count small since only genuinely-approaching signals surface), with the *"reasonable cadence, ~5/day"* ceiling living on the outbound/proactive-send path rather than the live render, as a ceiling not a target. Silence stays an acceptable output. (2) **A single "Working towards …" line in the header** names the goal that frames every recommendation — the one thing kept from the strip — or nothing when there's no goal yet. (3) **Removed the hardcoded `✓ variants selected / ✓ markdown applied` checklist** from `buildPrimaryMove`: the Shape B strip already stopped rendering it, but the template asserted work had happened regardless of what actually did, so it's deleted at source rather than left as a re-wireable landmine (`AGENTS.md:58`). `app/components/daily-home.tsx`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`.

- **The app-home header now wears the merchant's own brand logo.** The header mark was a fixed navy "J"; it now shows the store's real brand logo — `shop.brand.squareLogo` (it fits the square mark), falling back to `shop.brand.logo` — when the merchant has set one, and an honest store-initial monogram otherwise, never a fake photo (mirrors the ImageSlot rule). Fetched best-effort via Admin GraphQL in an isolated, `[]`-on-error query (so a scope gap or a store with no brand can never break the home), cached in `rawPayload.shopify.brandLogoUrl` — a free read on every load after the first, with the first-load fetch fire-and-forget off the LCP path (monogram until it persists, then the logo), the same pattern as the contact email. Resolved shape shared with the onboarding-email lane so the brand shows across app and inbox. `app/lib/shop/brand-logo.server.js`, `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`.

- **Opening Jefe always lands on the conversation now — plus two home-chat polish fixes.** (1) **Re-opening the app no longer drops the merchant back inside a move's chat.** The embedded app's URL is persistent (App Bridge restores the last location on re-open), so a move zoom (`?actionChat`) the merchant had left would silently re-open on the next visit — the first thing the founder hit in the new home. A fresh document load now drops a stale zoom param and lands on the home conversation; in-session zoom navigation is untouched, and a deliberate deep-link into a move can opt back in later. (2) **The composer no longer shows "Thinking" twice** — the Send button is a plain disabled state while sending, and the thinking row is the single indicator. (3) **Suggested openers under the composer** ("What changed this week?", "Anything I should worry about?", "How are my goals looking?") post the store-level chat, so an empty thread invites a real question rather than a blank box. `app/routes/app._index.tsx`, `app/components/daily-home.tsx`.

- **The chat composer is multi-line now.** The home and per-move composers were single-line inputs; they're now a shared textarea that auto-grows to its content (up to a cap, then scrolls) — **Enter sends, Shift+Enter inserts a newline**, and Enter is ignored mid-IME-composition so it's safe for non-Latin input. Extracted into one `ChatComposer` component so the home thread and the move zoom behave identically. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`.

- **Home chat: the composer clears on send, and the thread opens at the latest message.** Two fixes from the founder's live use. (1) The composer kept your text through the whole "Thinking" round-trip — it read as "the send didn't work" and invited a double-send. It now clears the moment the message is in flight (the optimistic copy is already in the thread), on both the home and the move zoom. (2) The conversation opened at its *oldest* exchange; it now opens anchored to the most recent message — before paint, so no visible jump — and follows new messages, unless you've scrolled up to read history (then it leaves you where you are). `app/components/daily-home.tsx`.

- **The home conversation now has a left-hand index of what's happened.** As the single thread accumulates, a long chat with no map gets unnavigable — so the home shows a rail of the *moments* (actions executed / reported-back / declined, and the current move), each of which **scrolls the thread to that message**. It's wayfinding *inside* the one conversation, never a thread picker: clicking scrolls, never swaps or navigates. Built only from real ledger data — no message-text scraping, no fabricated timestamps; goals and free-text corrections will join once their change-events are tagged. The rail appears once there's something to index (a fresh store stays a single centered column) and hides on narrow viewports so it never crowds the thread. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`.

- **The "See everything Jefe knows" link is off the home.** Per the founder, the quiet chat-log home shouldn't carry the Memory-view link. Removed it; the Merchant Memory surface and its `?view=memory` route are unchanged and still render — reachability is being re-homed (a settings/gear entry or a chat affordance) with the memory lane rather than sitting as a home footer link. `app/components/daily-home.tsx`.

- **Merchants can reach Settings — and set Jefe's autonomy — from a gear on the home.** A top-right gear on the home now opens the settings area (the home itself stays clean, chat-only, per the founder's call). Its first live section is **Autonomy**: the per-action recommend / approve / autonomous dials (live-vs-"Soon" from engine truth, not hardcoded), so a merchant can finally opt Jefe into acting on its own — or pull it back — the control PR #75's home rewrite had orphaned. Integrations, Channels and account Settings show an honest "coming soon" until their panels land.
